First, I recognize that the vertex of the parabola is at (0, 0), so the standard form of its equation will be either \( y^2 = 4ax \) for a horizontally opening parabola or \( x^2 = 4ay \) for a vertically opening parabola.

Given that the focus is at (0, 4), this indicates that the parabola opens upwards because the focus lies above the vertex. Therefore, the appropriate standard form is \( x^2 = 4ay \).

Substituting the value of 'a' from the focus coordinates:
- The formula for 'a' in terms of the focus (0, a) is known.
- Here, the focus is at (0, 4), so \( a = 4 \).

Plugging this into the standard equation:
\[ x^2 = 4 * 4y \]
\[ x^2 = 16y \]

Thus, the equation of the parabola with vertex at (0, 0) and focus at (0, 4) is \( x^2 = 16y \).

**Solution:**

We are given:
- **Vertex**: \((0, 0)\)
- **Focus**: \((0, 4)\)

To find the equation of the parabola, we can use the standard form of a parabola that opens upwards (since the focus is above the vertex):

\[ x^2 = 4ay \]

Here, \(a\) represents the distance from the vertex to the focus.

**Step 1:** Determine the value of \(a\).

Since the focus is at \((0, 4)\), we have:
\[ a = 4 \]

**Step 2:** Substitute \(a\) into the standard form equation.

\[ x^2 = 4(4)y \]
\[ x^2 = 16y \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{x^{2} = 16y}

The equation of the parabola with focus at \((\frac{5}{3}, 3)\) and directrix \(y = -1\) is:

$$
(x - \frac{5}{3})^2 = 8(y - 1)
$$

This represents a parabola that opens upwards, with its vertex located halfway between the focus and the directrix. The vertex is at \((\frac{5}{3}, 1)\), as derived from the standard form equation of a parabola.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Understand the Given Information:**
   - Focus (F): \((\frac{5}{3}, 3)\)
   - Directrix (D): \(y = -1\)

2. **Set Up the Distance Condition:**
   For any point \((x, y)\) on the parabola:
   - Distance to focus: \(\sqrt{(x - \frac{5}{3})^2 + (y - 3)^2}\)
   - Distance to directrix: \(|y - (-1)| = |y + 1|\)

   Since these distances are equal for a parabola:
   $$
   \sqrt{(x - \frac{5}{3})^2 + (y - 3)^2} = |y + 1|
   $$

3. **Square Both Sides to Eliminate Square Root:**
   $$
   (x - \frac{5}{3})^2 + (y - 3)^2 = (y + 1)^2
   $$

4. **Expand and Simplify the Equation:**
   - Expand left side:
     $$
     x^2 - \frac{10}{3}x + \frac{25}{9} + y^2 - 6y + 9 = y^2 + 2y + 1
     $$
   
   - Subtract \(y^2\) from both sides:
     $$
     x^2 - \frac{10}{3}x + \frac{25}{9} - 6y + 9 = 2y + 1
     $$

   - Bring all terms to the left side:
     $$
     x^2 - \frac{10}{3}x + \frac{25}{9} + 8 = 8y
     $$

   Simplify constants:
   $$
   x^2 - \frac{10}{3}x + \frac{97}{9} = 8y
   $$

5. **Complete the Square for the \(x\)-Terms:**
   - Coefficient of \(x\) is \(-\frac{10}{3}\).
   - Half of this coefficient is \(-\frac{5}{3}\), square it to get \(\frac{25}{9}\).

   Add \(\frac{25}{9}\) to both sides:
   $$
   x^2 - \frac{10}{3}x + \frac{25}{9} = 8y - \frac{72}{9}
   $$

6. **Express in Standard Parabola Form:**
   The left side is now a perfect square:
   $$
   (x - \frac{5}{3})^2 = 8(y - 1)
   $$

This final equation represents the parabola with the given focus and directrix, opening upwards.

First, I need to determine the equation of a parabola that passes through three given points: (2, 8), (-1, 3), and (0, 4). The general form of a quadratic equation is y = ax² + bx + c.

Since one of the points is (0, 4), I can immediately find the value of c by substituting x = 0 and y = 4 into the equation. This gives me c = 4.

Next, I'll substitute the remaining two points into the general form to create a system of equations:
For the point (2, 8):
8 = a(2)² + b(2) + 4 → 8 = 4a + 2b + 4

For the point (-1, 3):
3 = a(-1)² + b(-1) + 4 → 3 = a - b + 4

Now, I'll simplify these equations:
From (2, 8): 4a + 2b = 4
From (-1, 3): a - b = -1

I can solve this system of equations using substitution or elimination. Here, I'll use the elimination method.

First, multiply the second equation by 2 to make the coefficients of b equal:
2(a - b) = 2(-1) → 2a - 2b = -2

Now, add this to the first equation:
4a + 2b + 2a - 2b = 4 - 2
6a = 2 → a = 2/6 = 1/3

Substitute a = 1/3 into one of the simplified equations to find b. Using a - b = -1:
(1/3) - b = -1 → -b = -1 - (1/3) → -b = -4/3 → b = 4/3

Finally, with a = 1/3 and b = 4/3, the equation of the parabola is y = (1/3)x² + (4/3)x + 4.
</think>

To determine the equation of the parabola passing through the points \((2, 8)\), \((-1, 3)\), and \((0, 4)\), we start with the general form of a quadratic equation:

\[ y = ax^2 + bx + c \]

**Step 1: Find \(c\)**

Substitute the point \((0, 4)\) into the equation:
\[
4 = a(0)^2 + b(0) + c \\
\Rightarrow c = 4
\]

**Step 2: Substitute Remaining Points**

Using \(a\) and \(b\) with the remaining points.

For \((2, 8)\):
\[
8 = a(2)^2 + b(2) + 4 \\
8 = 4a + 2b + 4 \\
\Rightarrow 4a + 2b = 4 \quad \text{(Equation 1)}
\]

For \((-1, 3)\):
\[
3 = a(-1)^2 + b(-1) + 4 \\
3 = a - b + 4 \\
\Rightarrow a - b = -1 \quad \text{(Equation 2)}
\]

**Step 3: Solve the System of Equations**

From Equation 2:
\[
a = b - 1
\]

Substitute \(a\) into Equation 1:
\[
4(b - 1) + 2b = 4 \\
4b - 4 + 2b = 4 \\
6b = 8 \\
b = \frac{4}{3}
\]

Now, find \(a\):
\[
a = \frac{4}{3} - 1 = \frac{1}{3}
\]

**Step 4: Write the Final Equation**

Substitute \(a\) and \(b\) back into the general form:
\[
y = \frac{1}{3}x^2 + \frac{4}{3}x + 4
\]

The final equation of the parabola is:

\[
\boxed{\, y = \dfrac{1}{3}x^{2} + \dfrac{4}{3}x + 4 \,}
\]

To find the equation of the parabola with **vertex (2, -1)** and **directrix y = -4**, we follow these steps:

### Step 1: Determine the Direction of the Parabola
- The directrix is a horizontal line (**y = -4**), which means the parabola opens either **upward** or **downward**.
- Since the vertex (2, -1) lies above the directrix, the parabola opens **downward**.

### Step 2: Recall the Standard Form of a Vertical Parabola
The standard form of a vertical parabola with vertex (**h**, **k**) is:
\[
(y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h)
\]
- Here, (**h**, **k**) = (2, -1).
- The value of **p** represents the distance from the vertex to the focus and also from the vertex to the directrix.

### Step 3: Find the Value of p
- The directrix is at y = -4, and the vertex is at y = -1.
\[
\text{Distance between the vertex and the directrix} = |-1 - (-4)| = |3| = 3
\]
- Since the parabola opens downward, **p** will be negative:
\[
p = -3
\]

\]
Simplify it further:
\[
(y + 1)^2 = -12(x - 2)
\]

### Final Answer
The equation of the parabola is:
\[
\boxed{(y + 1)^2 = -12(x - 2)}
\]

To determine the **vertex** and other details of the parabola defined by the equation \( X^2 = -16y \), follow these steps:

### Understanding the Equation
The given equation is:
\[
X^2 = -16y
\]
This represents a vertical parabola. The standard form of such a parabola is:
\[
(X - h)^2 = 4p(y - k)
\]
where \((h, k)\) is the vertex and \( p \) determines the direction and width.

### Identifying the Vertex
Comparing \( X^2 = -16y \) with the standard form:

- There are no horizontal or vertical shifts (i.e., no terms like \(X - h\) or \(Y - k\)).
  
Thus, the vertex of the parabola is at:
\[
(h, k) = (0, 0)
\]

### Determining the Direction and Parameter \( p \)
From the equation \( X^2 = -16y \):

- It can be rewritten as:
  \[
  X^2 = 4(-4)y
  \]
  
Comparing this with the standard form \( (X - h)^2 = 4p(y - k) \), we find:
\[
4p = -16 \implies p = -4
\]

- **Direction**: Since \( p = -4 \), the parabola opens **downward**.

### Final Answer

The vertex of the parabola is at \( (0, 0) \), and it opens downward with a focal length of 4 units from the vertex.

First, I need to determine the center and radius of the circle. The endpoints of the diameter are given as (-2, 1) and (6, -3). 

To find the center, I'll calculate the midpoint between these two points using the midpoint formula:
\[
h = \frac{-2 + 6}{2} = 2 \\
k = \frac{1 + (-3)}{2} = -1
\]
So, the center of the circle is at (2, -1).

Next, I'll find the length of the radius. The radius is half the diameter, so I need to calculate the distance between the two given points first using the distance formula:
\[
d = \sqrt{(6 - (-2))^2 + (-3 - 1)^2} = \sqrt{8^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{64 + 16} = \sqrt{80}
\]
Therefore, the radius \( r \) is:
\[
r = \frac{\sqrt{80}}{2} = 2\sqrt{5}
\]

Finally, I'll write the equation of the circle using the standard form with the center (h, k) and radius r:
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = (2\sqrt{5})^2
\]
Simplifying \( (2\sqrt{5})^2 \):
\[
(2\sqrt{5})^2 = 4 \times 5 = 20
\]
So, the equation of the circle is:
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 20
\]
</think>

**Solution:**

To find the equation of the circle with endpoints of its diameter at points \((-2, 1)\) and \((6, -3)\), follow these steps:

1. **Find the Center (\(h, k\))**:
   
   The center is the midpoint of the diameter. Use the midpoint formula:
   \[
   h = \frac{-2 + 6}{2} = \frac{4}{2} = 2
   \]
   \[
   k = \frac{1 + (-3)}{2} = \frac{-2}{2} = -1
   \]
   
   So, the center is at \((2, -1)\).

2. **Find the Radius (\(r\))**:
   
   The radius is half the length of the diameter. First, calculate the distance between the two endpoints using the distance formula:
   \[
   d = \sqrt{(6 - (-2))^2 + (-3 - 1)^2} = \sqrt{8^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{64 + 16} = \sqrt{80}
   \]
   
   Then, divide by 2 to get the radius:
   \[
   r = \frac{\sqrt{80}}{2} = 2\sqrt{5}
   \]

3. **Write the Equation of the Circle**:
   
   The standard equation of a circle with center \((h, k)\) and radius \(r\) is:
   \[
   (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
   \]
   
   Substituting the values found:
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y - (-1))^2 = (2\sqrt{5})^2
   \]
   Simplify \(r^2\):
   \[
   (2\sqrt{5})^2 = 4 \times 5 = 20
   \]
   
   So, the equation of the circle is:
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 20
   \]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 20}
\]

On the right side: 12 + 9 is 21, plus 4 makes it 25. So now the equation is:

(x + 3)² + (y - 2)² = 25.

The radius r is the square root of 25, which is 5.

The given equation of the circle can be rewritten in standard form by completing the square for both x and y terms.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Start with the given equation:**
   \[
   X^2 + y^2 + 6x - 4y - 12 = 0
   \]
   
2. **Rearrange the terms to group like variables together:**
   \[
   X^2 + 6x + y^2 - 4y = 12
   \]
   
3. **Complete the square for the x terms:**
   - Coefficient of \(X\) is 6.
   - Take half of 6: \(3\).
   - Square it: \(3^2 = 9\).
   - Add 9 to both sides:
     \[
     X^2 + 6x + 9 = (X + 3)^2
     \]
   
4. **Complete the square for the y terms:**
   - Coefficient of \(y\) is -4.
   - Take half of -4: \(-2\).
   - Square it: \((-2)^2 = 4\).
   - Add 4 to both sides:
     \[
     y^2 - 4y + 4 = (y - 2)^2
     \]
   
5. **Add the constants to the right side of the equation:**
   \[
   (X + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 12 + 9 + 4
   \]
   \[
   (X + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25
   \]
   
6. **Express in standard form:**
   The equation is now in the form:
   \[
   (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
   \]
   where \(h = -3\), \(k = 2\), and \(r = 5\).

**Final Answer:**
The circle has a center at \((-3, 2)\) and a radius of \(5\).

   \[

To find the equation of the circle that is tangent to the x-axis and has its center at (3, 4), I start by recalling the standard form of a circle's equation.

The general equation of a circle with center (h, k) and radius r is:

$$(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2$$

In this case, the center of the circle is given as (3, 4), so I substitute h = 3 and k = 4 into the equation:

$$(x - 3)^2 + (y - 4)^2 = r^2$$

Next, since the circle is tangent to the x-axis, it touches the x-axis at exactly one point. The distance from the center of the circle to the x-axis must be equal to the radius of the circle.

The y-coordinate of the center is 4, which represents the vertical distance from the center to the x-axis (where y = 0). Therefore, the radius r of the circle is equal to this distance:

$$r = 4$$

Now that I know the radius, I can substitute it back into the equation of the circle:

$$(x - 3)^2 + (y - 4)^2 = 4^2$$
$$(x - 3)^2 + (y - 4)^2 = 16$$

Thus, the final equation of the circle is:

   The standard form of a circle's equation with center at the origin is:
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 = r^2
   \]
   where \( r \) is the radius.

   Since the circle passes through the point (0, 5), substitute these coordinates into the equation to find \( r \):
   \[
   (0)^2 + (5)^2 = r^2 \\
   0 + 25 = r^2 \\
   r^2 = 25
   \]

   \[
   x^2 + y^2 = 25
   \]

\[
(x - 1)^2 + (y + 2)^2 = 25
\]

We compare it to the standard form of a circle's equation:

\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]

Where:
- \((h, k)\) is the center of the circle.
- \(r\) is the radius.

- \(k = -2\)

The points where the line intersects the circle are calculated by solving the system of equations \( y = x + 3 \) and \( x^2 + y^2 = 17 \).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Substitute \( y \) from the linear equation into the circle's equation:**
   \[
   x^2 + (x + 3)^2 = 17
   \]
   
2. **Expand and simplify the equation:**
   \[
   x^2 + x^2 + 6x + 9 = 17 \\
   2x^2 + 6x - 8 = 0
   \]

3. **Divide by 2 to simplify:**
   \[
   x^2 + 3x - 4 = 0
   \]
   
4. **Solve the quadratic equation using the quadratic formula \( x = \frac{-b \pm \sqrt{b^2 - 4ac}}{2a} \), where \( a = 1 \), \( b = 3 \), and \( c = -4 \):**
   \[
   x = \frac{-3 \pm \sqrt{(3)^2 - 4(1)(-4)}}{2(1)} \\
   x = \frac{-3 \pm \sqrt{9 + 16}}{2} \\
   x = \frac{-3 \pm 5}{2}
   \]
   
5. **Find the two solutions for \( x \):**
   \[
   x_1 = \frac{-3 + 5}{2} = 1, \quad x_2 = \frac{-3 - 5}{2} = -4
   \]
   
6. **Substitute these \( x \)-values back into the linear equation to find the corresponding \( y \)-values:**
   - For \( x = 1 \): \( y = 1 + 3 = 4 \)
   - For \( x = -4 \): \( y = -4 + 3 = -1 \)

**Final Answer:** The points of intersection are (1, 4) and (-4, -1).

Now, to find D, E, and F in the circle's equation \( x^2 + y^2 + Dx + Ey + F = 0 \) passing through points (-1,2), (3,-4), and (5,6):

We set up three equations based on each point:

For (-1,2): \( (-1)^2 + 2^2 - D(1) + E(2) + F = 0 \Rightarrow 1 + 4 - D + 2E + F = 0 \Rightarrow -D + 2E + F = -5 \).

For (3,-4): \( 3^2 + (-4)^2 + 3D - 4E + F = 0 \Rightarrow 9 + 16 + 3D -4E + F = 0 \Rightarrow 3D - 4E + F = -25 \).

For (5,6): \( 5^2 + 6^2 + 5D + 6E + F = 0 \Rightarrow 25 + 36 + 5D + 6E + F = 0 \Rightarrow 5D + 6E + F = -61 \).

Solving these equations step by step:

From the first equation, express \( F = D - 2E - 5 \).

Substitute \( F \) into the second and third equations:

Second equation becomes:  
\( 3D -4E + (D - 2E - 5) = -25 \Rightarrow 4D -6E = -20 \) → Simplify to \( 2D - 3E = -10 \).

Third equation becomes:  
\( 5D +6E + (D - 2E -5) =-61 \Rightarrow 6D +4E =-56 \) → Simplify to \( 3D +2E = -28 \).

Solve the system of equations:
1. \( 2D -3E = -10 \)
2. \( 3D +2E = -28 \)

Multiply equation 1 by 3 and equation 2 by 2 to eliminate D:

- \( 6D -9E = -30 \)
- \( 6D +4E = -56 \)

Subtract the second from the first:  
\( -13E = 26 \Rightarrow E = -2 \).

Substitute \( E = -2 \) into equation 1:  
\( 2D -3(-2) = -10 \Rightarrow 2D +6 =-10 \Rightarrow D=-8 \).

Finally, substitute \( D = -8 \) and \( E = -2 \) into the first expression for F:  
\( F = (-8) -2*(-2) -5 = -8 +4 -5 = -9 \).

**Answer:**  
D is \(-8\), E is \(-2\), and F is \(-9\).

The given equation of the circle is rearranged by completing the square for both x and y terms.

1. **Rearrange the equation**:  
   X² - 8x + y² - 6y = -20

2. **Complete the square**:
   - For x: Take half of -8 (which is -4), square it to get 16, add and subtract this value.
   - For y: Take half of -6 (which is -3), square it to get 9, add and subtract this value.

3. **Rewrite the equation** with completed squares:
   (X - 4)² + (Y - 3)² = 5

From this standard form, we can identify the center as (4, 3) and the radius as √5.

- Radius: √5

The standard form of an ellipse centered at the origin with a horizontal major axis is given by:

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

where:
- The length of the major axis (horizontal) is \(2a\), so if it's 10, then \(a = 5\).
- The length of the minor axis (vertical) is \(2b\), so if it's 6, then \(b = 3\).

Substituting these values into the standard equation gives:

\[
\frac{x^2}{5^2} + \frac{y^2}{3^2} = 1
\]

Simplifying this, we get:

\[
\frac{x^2}{25} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
\]

The given equation \(9x^2 + 4y^2 = 36\) can be converted into the standard form of an ellipse by dividing both sides by 36. This results in \(\frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1\).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Start with the given equation:** \(9x^2 + 4y^2 = 36\).
2. **Divide each term by 36** to normalize the equation:
   - For the x-term: \(\frac{9x^2}{36} = \frac{x^2}{4}\)
   - For the y-term: \(\frac{4y^2}{36} = \frac{y^2}{9}\)
   - The right side becomes \(\frac{36}{36} = 1\).
3. **Write the equation in standard form:** Combining these, we get \(\frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1\).

The given equation of the ellipse is \( \frac{x^2}{36} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \).

Here, \( a^2 = 36 \) so \( a = 6 \), and \( b^2 = 9 \) so \( b = 3 \). The eccentricity \( e \) is calculated as:

\[
e = \frac{\sqrt{a^2 - b^2}}{a} = \frac{\sqrt{36 - 9}}{6} = \frac{\sqrt{27}}{6} = \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{6} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}
\]

To determine the condition on \( h \) and \( k \) such that the given ellipse and hyperbola share common tangent lines, we start by considering the equations of the tangent lines for both conic sections.

For an ellipse given by \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\), the equation of a tangent line is:
\[ y = mx \pm \sqrt{a^2m^2 + b^2} \]

For a hyperbola given by \(\frac{x^2}{h^2} - \frac{y^2}{k^2} = 1\), the equation of a tangent line is:
\[ y = mx \pm \sqrt{h^2m^2 - k^2} \]

For these lines to be common tangents, their constants must match. Therefore, we equate the constants from both equations:
\[ \sqrt{a^2m^2 + b^2} = \sqrt{h^2m^2 - k^2} \]

Squaring both sides to eliminate the square roots, we get:
\[ a^2m^2 + b^2 = h^2m^2 - k^2 \]

Rearranging terms, we have:
\[ (h^2 - a^2)m^2 = b^2 + k^2 \]

For this equation to have real solutions for \( m \), the coefficient of \( m^2 \) must be positive. Therefore, we require:
\[ h^2 - a^2 > 0 \]

This simplifies to:
\[ h^2 > a^2 \]

Thus, the necessary condition for the hyperbola and ellipse to share common tangent lines is that \( h^2 \) must exceed \( a^2 \).

\[
\boxed{h^2 > a^2}
\]

***********************<think>
To find the area of an ellipse given by the equation \( \frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \), I first recall that the standard form of an ellipse is \( \frac{(x-h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y-k)^2}{b^2} = 1 \), where (h, k) is the center, and a and b are the semi-major and semi-minor axes.

In this equation, the denominators under x² and y² are 4 and 9, respectively. Therefore, \( a^2 = 4 \) so \( a = 2 \), and \( b^2 = 9 \) so \( b = 3 \). The area of an ellipse is given by the formula \( \pi ab \).

Substituting the values, the area becomes \( \pi \times 2 \times 3 = 6\pi \).
</think>

To find the **area** of the ellipse given by the equation:

\[
\frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
\]

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Identify the Standard Form of an Ellipse:**
   
   The standard form of an ellipse centered at the origin is:
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]
   where \( a \) and \( b \) are the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes, respectively.

2. **Compare with Given Equation:**
   
   Compare the given equation:
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
   \]
   with the standard form to identify \( a^2 \) and \( b^2 \):
   - \( a^2 = 4 \Rightarrow a = 2 \)
   - \( b^2 = 9 \Rightarrow b = 3 \)

3. **Calculate the Area of the Ellipse:**
   
   The area \( A \) of an ellipse is given by:
   \[
   A = \pi \times a \times b
   \]
   Substitute \( a = 2 \) and \( b = 3 \):
   \[
   A = \pi \times 2 \times 3 = 6\pi
   \]

### Final Answer:

\[
\boxed{\text{Area} = 6\pi}
\]

Alright, so I need to find the equation of an ellipse centered at (-2, 1). The major axis is horizontal and has a length of 12, while the minor axis is vertical with a length of 8. Hmm, okay.

First, I remember that the standard form of an ellipse's equation depends on whether it's oriented horizontally or vertically. Since the major axis is horizontal, the standard form should be:

\[
\frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Where (h, k) is the center of the ellipse. In this case, h is -2 and k is 1, so plugging those in gives:

\[
\frac{(x + 2)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y - 1)^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Now, I need to find the values of a² and b². The lengths given are for the major and minor axes, so first I have to remember that these lengths are twice the semi-major axis (a) and semi-minor axis (b). 

The major axis is 12 units long, which means the full length is 2a. Therefore, solving for a gives:

\[
2a = 12 \Rightarrow a = 6
\]

Similarly, the minor axis is 8 units, so:

\[
2b = 8 \Rightarrow b = 4
\]

Now I can square these to get a² and b². 

Calculating a²: 

\[
a^2 = 6^2 = 36
\]

And b²:

\[
b^2 = 4^2 = 16
\]

So now, substituting back into the standard equation with h=-2 and k=1:

\[
\frac{(x + 2)^2}{36} + \frac{(y - 1)^2}{16} = 1
\]
</think>

The equation of the ellipse centered at (-2, 1) with a horizontal major axis length of 12 and a vertical minor axis length of 8 is:

\[
\frac{(x + 2)^2}{36} + \frac{(y - 1)^2}{16} = 1
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Center:** The center of the ellipse is given as (-2, 1). This means h = -2 and k = 1 in the standard equation.

2. **Determine the Major Axis Length (2a):** The length of the major axis is 12, so half of that (the semi-major axis) is a = 6. Therefore, \( a^2 = 36 \).

3. **Determine the Minor Axis Length (2b):** The length of the minor axis is 8, so half of that (the semi-minor axis) is b = 4. Thus, \( b^2 = 16 \).

4. **Substitute into the Standard Equation:** Plugging h, k, a², and b² into the standard form equation gives:

\[
\frac{(x + 2)^2}{36} + \frac{(y - 1)^2}{16} = 1
\]

**Final Answer:**
The equation of the ellipse is \boxed{\dfrac{(x+2)^2}{36} + \dfrac{(y-1)^2}{16} = 1}.

Alright, so I need to find the foci and the vertices (which are also called the endpoints) of an ellipse given by the equation \((X - 1)^2/9 + (y + 2)^2/16 = 1\). Hmm, okay. Let me recall what I know about ellipses.

First off, the general form of an ellipse is \(\frac{(x-h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y-k)^2}{b^2} = 1\) if it's wider horizontally or \( \frac{(x-h)^2}{b^2} + \frac{(y-k)^2}{a^2} = 1 \) if it's taller vertically. Here, (h,k) is the center of the ellipse.

To determine the **eccentricity** (\(e\)) of the given hyperbola:

### Given:
The equation of the hyperbola is:
\[ \frac{y^2}{25} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1 \]

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Identify Standard Form:**
   The general form of a vertical hyperbola (where the transverse axis is along the y-axis) is:
   \[ \frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(x - h)^2}{b^2} = 1 \]
   
   Comparing with the given equation, we have:
   \[
   a^2 = 25 \\
   b^2 = 16
   \]

2. **Calculate Eccentricity:**
   The formula for eccentricity (\(e\)) of a hyperbola is:
   \[ e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{b^2}{a^2}} \]
   
   Plugging in the values:
   \[
   e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{16}{25}} \\
   e = \sqrt{\frac{41}{25}} \\
   e = \frac{\sqrt{41}}{5}
   \]

### Final Answer:
The eccentricity of the hyperbola is:

\[
\boxed{e = \dfrac{\sqrt{41}}{5}}
\]

- **Vertices**: (0, ±3) imply that \( a = 3 \).
- **Foci**: (0, ±5) imply that \( c = 5 \).

For hyperbolas, the relationship between \( a \), \( b \), and \( c \) is given by:

I remember that hyperbolas can be written in two main forms: one where the transverse axis is horizontal, like this one, and another where it's vertical. The given equation has x squared over something minus y squared over something, so it must be a horizontally oriented hyperbola.

The standard form for a horizontally oriented hyperbola is (x - h)²/a² - (y - k)²/b² = 1, where the center is at (h, k). In this case, our equation is x²/4 - y²/9 = 1, so h and k are both zero. That means the center of the hyperbola is at the origin (0,0).

1. **Identify the Orientation:**
   Since both vertices and foci are on the y-axis, this is a vertical hyperbola of the form:
   \[
   \frac{y^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]

2. **Determine \( a \):**
   The vertices are at (0, 5) and (0, -3). The distance from the center to each vertex is:
   \[
   a = 4 \quad (\text{since } |5| = 4)
   \]
   
3. **Determine \( c \):**
   The foci are at (0, -5) and (0, 3). The distance from the center to each focus is:
   \[
   c = 4 \quad (\text{since } |5| = 4)
   \]

   For hyperbolas, the relationship between \( a \), \( b \), and \( c \) is:

   \]

   \[

To determine the equation of the parabola with a **vertex** at \((3, 4)\) and a **directrix** at \(y = 2\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Identify the Direction of the Parabola
- The directrix is a horizontal line (\(y = 2\)), which means the parabola opens either upward or downward.
- Since the vertex \((3, 4)\) lies above the directrix \(y = 2\), the parabola **opens upward**.

### Step 2: Determine the Value of \(p\)
- The distance between the vertex and the directrix is equal to \(|p|\).
- Given that the vertex is at \((3, 4)\) and the directrix is at \(y = 2\):
  \[
  p = 4 - 2 = 2
  \]
  
### Step 3: Use the Standard Form of a Parabola's Equation
For a parabola that opens upward with vertex \((h, k)\), the standard form is:
\[
(y - k) = \frac{1}{4p}(x - h)^2
\]

Plugging in \(h = 3\), \(k = 4\), and \(p = 2\):
\[
y - 4 = \frac{1}{8}(x - 3)^2
\]

(x - 2)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = (x + 1)^2

(x^2 - 4x + 4)

(y^2 - 6y + 9)

x^2 + 2x + 1

Final Answer:
Equation of Parabola: {Final Answer: "(y - 3)^2 = 6(x - 0.5)"}

Step-by-Step Explanation:

1. **Distance from (x, y) to Focus (2, 3):**  
   Using the distance formula: sqrt[(x - 2)^2 + (y - 3)^2].

2. **Distance from (x, y) to Directrix x = -1:**  
   The perpendicular distance is |x + 1|.

3. **Set Distances Equal and Square Both Sides:**  
   sqrt[(x - 2)^2 + (y - 3)^2] = |x + 1|. Squaring both sides gives:  
   (x - 2)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = (x + 1)^2.

4. **Expand and Simplify:**  
   Expanding leads to x^2 - 4x + 4 + y^2 - 6y + 9 = x^2 + 2x + 1.  
   Simplifying further: y^2 - 6x - 6y + 12 = 0.

5. **Complete the Square for Y Terms:**  
   (y - 3)^2 = 6(x - 0.5).

This represents a horizontally opening parabola with vertex at (0.5, 3) and focus at (2, 3), consistent with given directrix x=-1.

To find the vertex form of the parabola given by Y = 3x² + 6x + 5, I'll start by recalling that the vertex form is useful for identifying the vertex of a parabola easily.

First, I need to complete the square for the quadratic expression in standard form. The general approach involves factoring out the coefficient of x² from the first two terms and then adjusting the constant term to maintain equality.

So, starting with Y = 3x² + 6x + 5, I'll factor out a 3 from the first two terms: Y = 3(x² + 2x) + 5. Next, I need to determine what number to add inside the parentheses so that it becomes a perfect square trinomial. This number is (b/2)² where b is the coefficient of x. In this case, b is 2, so (2/2)² = 1.

Adding and subtracting 1 inside the parentheses gives me Y = 3[(x² + 2x + 1) - 1] + 5. Simplifying further, I get Y = 3(x + 1)² - 3 + 5, which simplifies to Y = 3(x + 1)² + 2.

Thus, the vertex form of the given equation is Y = 3(x + 1)² + 2.
</think>

To find the **vertex** (also known as the **turning point**) of the parabola defined by the equation \( Y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \), we'll use the method of **completing the square** to convert it into vertex form. 

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Start with the given equation:**
   \[
   Y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5
   \]

2. **Factor out the coefficient of \( x^2 \) from the first two terms:**
   \[
   Y = 3(x^2 + 2x) + 5
   \]

3. **Complete the square inside the parentheses:**
   - Take half of the coefficient of \( x \): \( \frac{2}{2} = 1 \)
   - Square it: \( (1)^2 = 1 \)

4. **Add and subtract this value inside the parentheses to maintain equality:**
   \[
   Y = 3\left( x^2 + 2x + 1 - 1 \right) + 5
   \]
   Simplify:
   \[
   Y = 3\left( (x + 1)^2 - 1 \right) + 5
   \]

5. **Distribute the 3 and combine like terms:**
   \[
   Y = 3(x + 1)^2 - 3 + 5
   \]
   \[
   Y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2
   \]

### Final Answer:

The equation in vertex form is:
\[
Y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2
\]
So, the **vertex** (shifting point) of the parabola is at \( (-1, 2) \).

To find the equation of the parabola with vertex at \((0, 3)\) and directrix \(y = 3\), follow these steps:

1. **Identify the Direction of the Parabola:**
   Since the directrix is a horizontal line (\(y = 3\)) and it lies above the vertex \((0, 3)\), the parabola opens **downward**.

2. **Determine the Standard Form:**
   The standard form of a downward-opening parabola with vertex \((h, k)\) is:
   \[
   y = -a(x - h)^2 + k
   \]
   Here, \(h = 0\) and \(k = 3\), so the equation becomes:
   \[
   y = -a x^2 + 3
   \]

3. **Find the Value of 'a':**
   The distance between the vertex and the directrix (\(p\)) is 1 unit (since from \(y = 3\) to itself, but considering standard parabola properties, we use absolute value).

The equation of the circle with endpoints (-2, 1) and (6, -3) as its diameter can be found by first determining the midpoint (center) and then calculating the radius.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Find the Center (Midpoint):**
   Use the midpoint formula:
   \[
   \left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right)
   \]
   Plugging in (-2, 1) and (6, -3):
   \[
   \left( \frac{-2 + 6}{2}, \frac{1 + (-3)}{2} \right) = (2, -1)
   \]

2. **Calculate the Radius:**
   Determine the length of the diameter using the distance formula:
   \[
   \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
   \]
   Substituting the points:
   \[
   \sqrt{(6 - (-2))^2 + (-3 - 1)^2} = \sqrt{8^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{64 + 16} = \sqrt{80} = 4\sqrt{5}
   \]
   The radius is half the diameter:
   \[
   r = \frac{4\sqrt{5}}{2} = 2\sqrt{5}
   \]

3. **Write the Equation of the Circle:**
   Using the standard form \((x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2\) where \(h = 2\), \(k = -1\), and \(r = 2\sqrt{5}\):
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y - (-1))^2 = (2\sqrt{5})^2
   \]
   Simplify:
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 20
   \]

**Final Answer:**
The equation of the circle is \(\boxed{(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 20}\).

1. Start with the given equation:
   \[
   X^2 + Y^2 + 6X - 4Y - 12 = 0
   \]

2. Rearrange terms to group \( X \) and \( Y \):
   \[
   (X^2 + 6X) + (Y^2 - 4Y) = 12
   \]

3. Complete the square for \( X \) and \( Y \):

     \[
     X^2 + 6X + 9 = (X + 3)^2
     \]
   - For \( Y \): Add and subtract \( (-4/2)^2 = 4 \).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Circle's Center:** The problem states that the center is at the origin, which has coordinates (0,0).

2. **Use the General Equation of a Circle:** The standard equation for a circle with center (h,k) and radius r is:
   \[
   (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
   \]
   Since the center is at (0,0), substitute h=0 and k=0 into the equation:
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 = r^2
   \]

   0 + 25 = r^2 \\
   r^2 = 25
   \]

   \[

   \[

1. For (1, 1):

2. For (2, -3):
\( (2 - h)^2 + (-3 - k)^2 = r^2 \) --- Equation 2

3. For (5, -1):
\( (5 - h)^2 + (-1 - k)^2 = r^2 \) --- Equation 3

Equation 1: \( (1 - h)^2 + (1 - k)^2 = r^2 \)

Equation 2: \( (2 - h)^2 + (-3 - k)^2 = r^2 \)

Equation 3: \( (5 - h)^2 + (-1 - k)^2 = r^2 \)

Since all three equal \( r^2 \), I can set them equal to each other to eliminate \( r^2 \).

Let's subtract Equation 1 from Equation 2:

\( [ (2 - h)^2 + (-3 - k)^2 ] - [ (1 - h)^2 + (1 - k)^2 ] = 0 \)

(-3 -k)^2 = (9 + 6k + k^2)

(1 - k)^2 = (1 - 2k + k^2)

First term in Equation 2 minus first term in Equation 1:

(4 -4h + h^2) - (1 -2h + h^2) = 4 -4h + h^2 -1 +2h -h^2 = (4-1) + (-4h +2h) + (h² - h²) = 3 - 2h

Given the equation \( x^2 + y^2 = 1 \), we recognize that it represents a circle of radius 1 centered at the origin. To express the solutions parametrically, we can use trigonometric functions.

The parametric equations for this circle are:
\[ x = \cos t \]
\[ y = \sin t \]

Thus, the solutions can be written as:
\[
(x(t), y(t)) = (\cos t, \sin t)
\]

Therefore, the final answer is:

\[
\boxed{(x(t), y(t)) = (\cos t, \sin t)}
\]

The given equation of the circle can be rewritten in standard form by completing the square. 

First, rearrange the terms:
\( x^2 - 8x + y^2 - 6y = -20 \)

Complete the square for \( x \):
Take half of -8, which is -4, and square it to get 16.

Complete the square for \( y \):
Take half of -6, which is -3, and square it to get 9.

Add these squares to both sides:
\( (x^2 - 8x + 16) + (y^2 - 6y + 9) = -20 + 16 + 9 \)

Simplify:
\( (x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = 5 \)

From this, the center is at \( (4, 3) \) and the radius is \( \sqrt{5} \).

Final Answer: Center is (4, 3), Radius is √5.

- Minor axis length (2b): 8 ⇒ b = 4

To convert the given equation into the standard form of an ellipse, follow these steps:

1. **Start with the given equation:**
   
   \[
   9x^2 + 4y^2 = 36
   \]

2. **Divide each term by 36 to simplify and get it in the form \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\):**

   \[

***********************<think>
First, I need to determine the type of conic section represented by the given equation. The equation is \( \frac{x^2}{36} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \).

Since both denominators are positive and under squared terms, this represents an ellipse.

Next, identify the major and minor axes:

- The denominator under \( x^2 \) (36) is larger than that under \( y^2 \) (9), so the major axis is along the x-axis.
  
Now, calculate the necessary parameters for the ellipse:

1. **Calculate a**:
   - \( a = \sqrt{A} \)
   - Here, \( A = 36 \)
   - So, \( a = \sqrt{36} = 6 \)

2. **Calculate b**:
   - \( b = \sqrt{B} \)
   - Here, \( B = 9 \)
   - So, \( b = \sqrt{9} = 3 \)

Finally, compute the eccentricity (e):

- Use the formula: \( e = \sqrt{1 - \frac{b^2}{a^2}} \)
- Plugging in the values:
  - \( e = \sqrt{1 - \frac{9}{36}} = \sqrt{\frac{27}{36}} = \sqrt{\frac{3}{4}} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \)

Thus, the eccentricity of the ellipse is \( \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \).

To determine the **eccentricity** of the given ellipse:

1. **Identify the standard form of the ellipse equation:**
   
   The general form for an ellipse centered at the origin with major axis along the x-axis is:
   
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]
   
   Where:
   - \( a \) is the semi-major axis.
   - \( b \) is the semi-minor axis.

2. **Compare with the given equation:**
   
   Given:
   
   \[

   \]
   
   Here, \( a^2 = 36 \) and \( b^2 = 9 \).

   - \( b = \sqrt{9} = 3 \)

   \]
   
   Plugging in the values:

\[

\frac{9}{a^2} = 1 \\
a^2 = 9 \\
a = 3
\]

**Step 2: Determine \( b \)**

Using the point (0, 2):
\[
\frac{0^2}{3^2} + \frac{2^2}{b^2} = 1 \\
\frac{4}{b^2} = 1 \\
b^2 = 4 \\
b = 2
\]

**Final Equation of the Ellipse**

Substituting \( a^2 = 9 \) and \( b^2 = 4 \):
\[
\boxed{\dfrac{x^2}{9} + \dfrac{y^2}{4} = 1}
\]

To determine the eccentricity of the hyperbola given by the equation \( \frac{y^2}{25} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1 \), I start by identifying the standard form of a hyperbola centered at the origin.

Since the y-term is positive, this is a vertical hyperbola. The general form for such hyperbolas is \( \frac{y^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1 \), where \( a^2 = 25 \) and \( b^2 = 16 \).

Next, I calculate the values of \( a \) and \( b \):
- \( a = \sqrt{25} = 5 \)
- \( b = \sqrt{16} = 4 \)

For hyperbolas, the eccentricity \( e \) is given by the formula:
\[ e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{b^2}{a^2}} \]

Substituting the known values into this formula allows me to compute the eccentricity accurately.
</think>

**Solution:**

To find the **eccentricity (विलक्षणता)** of the hyperbola given by the equation:
\[
\frac{y^2}{25} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1
\]

**Step 1:** Identify the standard form of the hyperbola.

The given equation is in the standard form of a **vertical hyperbola** (opens upwards and downwards) centered at the origin:
\[
\frac{y^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1
\]
where \( a^2 = 25 \) and \( b^2 = 16 \).

**Step 2:** Calculate \( a \) and \( b \).

\[
a = \sqrt{a^2} = \sqrt{25} = 5
\]
\[
b = \sqrt{b^2} = \sqrt{16} = 4
\]

**Step 3:** Use the formula for eccentricity of a hyperbola.

The eccentricity (\( e \)) is given by:
\[
e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{b^2}{a^2}}
\]

Substitute \( a^2 = 25 \) and \( b^2 = 16 \):
\[
e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{16}{25}} = \sqrt{\frac{41}{25}} = \frac{\sqrt{41}}{5}
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\text{Final Answer: }\frac{\sqrt{41}}{5}}
\]

Alright, for a standard hyperbola of the form (x - h)²/a² - (y - k)²/b² = 1, the center is at (h, k). In this case, our equation doesn't have any shifts since it's just x²/4 and y²/9. So h and k are both zero; the center is at the origin, (0,0).

Next, I need to find the vertices. The vertices are located a units away from the center along the transverse axis. Since this is horizontal, they'll be on the x-axis. Here, a² is 4, so a is 2. Therefore, the vertices are at (±a, 0), which is (±2, 0). Got that down.

Now, moving on to the foci. Foci are located c units away from the center, where c² = a² + b². I have a² as 4 and b² as 9, so c squared should be 4 plus 9, which is 13. Taking the square root of that gives c equals sqrt(13). So the foci are at (±sqrt(13), 0). Since it's along the x-axis.

Finally, asymptotes for a hyperbola. They're the lines that the hyperbola approaches but never touches. For horizontal hyperbolas, the equations of the asymptotes are y = ±(b/a)x plus any shift (but again, in this case, no shift). Here, b is 3 and a is 2, so the slopes are ±3/2. Therefore, the equations are y = (3/2)x and y = -(3/2)x.

Let me just make sure I didn't mix up any formulas. The standard form is correct for horizontal hyperbola, yes. The center is indeed at (0,0). Vertices are along a², which is 4, so yeah, ±2 on the x-axis. Foci use c squared equals a squared plus b squared, so that's 4+9=13, giving sqrt(13) for foci distance.

Asymptotes: y = (b/a)x and y = -(b/a)x because it's horizontal. So with b=3 and a=2, slopes are ±3/2. Yep, that all lines up. 

I think I have everything covered here. Vertices at (±2,0), foci at (±sqrt(13), 0), asymptotes y = ±(3/2)x.
</think>

The given hyperbola equation is \( \frac{x^2}{4} - \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \). 

- **Center**: (0, 0)
- **Vertices**: Since \( a^2 = 4 \), \( a = 2 \), so the vertices are at \( (\pm2, 0) \).
- **Foci**: Using \( c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \), where \( a^2 = 4 \) and \( b^2 = 9 \), we get \( c^2 = 13 \). Thus, \( c = \sqrt{13} \), so the foci are at \( (\pm\sqrt{13}, 0) \).
- **Asymptotes**: The equations are \( y = \pm\frac{b}{a}x \), which simplifies to \( y = \pm\frac{3}{2}x \).

1. From the focus: \(h + p = 2\)

Next, I solve this system of equations to find the values of h and p.

Adding both equations gives \(2h = 1\), so \(h = \frac{1}{2}\).

Substituting \(h\) back into the first equation:  
\(\frac{1}{2} + p = 2\)  
Solving for \(p\) yields \(p = \frac{3}{2}\).

\[

To find the equation of the parabola with **focus (2, 3)** and **directrix \( x = -1 \)**, follow these steps:

### Step 1: Understand the Properties
- **Focus**: The point from which all points on the parabola are equidistant.
- **Directrix**: The line that forms the same distance with any point on the parabola as the focus.

For a parabola opening horizontally, the standard form is:
\[
(y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h)
\]
Where:
- \((h + p, k)\) is the focus.
- \(x = h - p\) is the equation of the directrix.

### Step 2: Identify Key Components
From the given information:
- **Focus (2, 3)** implies:
  \[
  h + p = 2 \\
  k = 3
  \]
- **Directrix \( x = -1 \)** implies:
  \[
  h - p = -1
  \]

### Step 3: Solve for \(h\) and \(p\)
Set up the equations:
\[
h + p = 2 \\
h - p = -1
\]
Adding both equations:
\[
2h = 1 \implies h = \frac{1}{2}
\]
Substitute \( h \) back into one of the original equations to find \( p \):
\[
\frac{1}{2} + p = 2 \\
p = \frac{3}{2}
\]

### Step 4: Plug Values into Standard Form
Now that we have \( h = \frac{1}{2} \), \( k = 3 \), and \( p = \frac{3}{2} \), substitute these into the standard form:
\[
(y - 3)^2 = 4 \times \frac{3}{2}(x - \frac{1}{2})
\]
Simplify:
\[
(y - 3)^2 = 6(x - \frac{1}{2})
\]

### Final Answer: 
The equation of the parabola is:
\[
\boxed{(y - 3)^2 = 6\left(x - \frac{1}{2}\right)}
\]

### Step 1: Start with the Given Equation
\[ x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \]

### Step 2: Rearrange the Equation to Isolate \( y \)
Subtract \( x^2 - 4x \) and 12 from both sides:
\[ 8y = -x^2 + 4x - 12 \]

Divide both sides by 8 to solve for \( y \):
\[ y = -\frac{1}{8}x^2 + \frac{1}{2}x - 1.5 \]

### Step 3: Identify the Quadratic and Linear Coefficients
The equation is now in the form:
\[ y = ax^2 + bx + c \]
Where:
- \( a = -\frac{1}{8} \)
- \( b = \frac{1}{2} \)
- \( c = -1.5 \)

### Step 4: Find the Vertex (Vertex Form)
The vertex form of a parabola is:
\[ y = a(x - h)^2 + k \]
Where \( (h, k) \) is the vertex.

To find \( h \):
\[ h = -\frac{b}{2a} = -\frac{\frac{1}{2}}{2 \times -\frac{1}{8}} = 2 \]

Substitute \( h = 2 \) and \( k = -1 \):
\[ y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 - 1 \]

### Final Answer
The equation in standard form is:

Given a focus at \((0, 3)\) and a directrix \(y = 3\), we need to determine if there exists a non-degenerate parabola. 

A parabola is defined as the set of all points equidistant from a fixed point (the focus) and a fixed line (the directrix). 

For any point \((x, y)\) on the parabola, the distance to the focus \((0, 3)\) must equal the distance to the directrix \(y = 3\). 

1. The distance from \((x, y)\) to the focus \((0, 3)\) is given by:
   \[
   \sqrt{x^2 + (y - 3)^2}
   \]

2. The distance from \((x, y)\) to the directrix \(y = 3\) is given by:
   \[
   |y - 3|
   \]

Setting these distances equal gives:
\[
\sqrt{x^2 + (y - 3)^2} = |y - 3|
\]

Squaring both sides to eliminate the square root:
\[
x^2 + (y - 3)^2 = (y - 3)^2
\]

Simplifying this equation:
\[
x^2 = 0
\]

Thus, \(x = 0\), which is a vertical line. This indicates that all points satisfying the condition lie on the vertical line \(x = 0\).

Therefore, there is no non-degenerate parabola in this case as it reduces to the straight line \(x = 0\). The equation of such a degenerate "parabola" is:

\[
\boxed{x = 0}
\]

Next, I need to find how far the focus is from the vertex. The formula for the distance between the vertex and the focus (also known as p) in a parabola is given by p = 1/(4a). Plugging in a = 1 here, we get p = 1/(4*1) = 1/4 or 0.25 units.

Since the parabola opens upwards and the vertex is at (1, 5), the focus will be located 'p' units above the vertex along the axis of symmetry. The axis of symmetry for this parabola is a vertical line that passes through the vertex, so it's x = h, which in this case is x = 1.

So adding p to the y-coordinate of the vertex, the focus will be at (h, k + p) = (1, 5 + 0.25) = (1, 5.25).

To determine the equation of a parabola passing through the points (2,8), (-1,3), and (0,4), I'll use the standard form of a quadratic equation: y = ax² + bx + c.

First, substituting each point into the equation gives me three equations:
- For (2,8): 8 = 4a + 2b + c
- For (-1,3): 3 = a - b + c
- For (0,4): 4 = c

From the third equation, I immediately know that c is 4.

Substituting c into the first two equations:
- 8 = 4a + 2b + 4 → Simplify to 4a + 2b = 4
- 3 = a - b + 4 → Simplify to a - b = -1

Now, I'll solve this system of equations. From the second simplified equation:
a = b - 1

Substituting into the first equation:
4(b - 1) + 2b = 4
Expanding gives: 4b - 4 + 2b = 4 → 6b - 4 = 4 → 6b = 8 → b = 8/6 = 4/3

Then, a = (4/3) - 1 = 1/3.

Therefore, the equation of the parabola is y = (1/3)x² + (4/3)x + 4.
</think>

To determine the equation of a parabola passing through the points \((2,8)\), \((-1,3)\), and \((0,4)\), we'll use the standard form of a quadratic equation:

\[ y = ax^2 + bx + c \]

### Step 1: Set Up Equations
Substitute each point into the general equation to create three equations:

1. For \((2,8)\):
   \[ 8 = a(2)^2 + b(2) + c \]
   \[ 8 = 4a + 2b + c \]

2. For \((-1,3)\):
   \[ 3 = a(-1)^2 + b(-1) + c \]
   \[ 3 = a - b + c \]

3. For \((0,4)\):
   \[ 4 = a(0)^2 + b(0) + c \]
   \[ 4 = c \]

### Step 2: Solve for \(c\)
From the third equation:
\[ c = 4 \]

### Step 3: Substitute \(c\) into Remaining Equations
1. From the first equation:
   \[ 8 = 4a + 2b + 4 \]
   \[ 4a + 2b = 4 \quad \text{(Equation A)} \]

2. From the second equation:
   \[ 3 = a - b + 4 \]
   \[ a - b = -1 \quad \text{(Equation B)} \]

### Step 4: Solve Equations A and B
From Equation B:
\[ a = b - 1 \]

Substitute \(a\) into Equation A:
\[ 4(b - 1) + 2b = 4 \]
\[ 4b - 4 + 2b = 4 \]
\[ 6b - 4 = 4 \]
\[ 6b = 8 \]
\[ b = \frac{4}{3} \]

Then,
\[ a = \frac{4}{3} - 1 = \frac{1}{3} \]

### Step 5: Write the Equation of the Parabola
Substituting \(a\), \(b\), and \(c\) back into the general equation:
\[ y = \frac{1}{3}x^2 + \frac{4}{3}x + 4 \]

**Final Answer:**  
The equation of the parabola is:

\[
\boxed{\text{Final Answer: } y = \frac{1}{3}x^2 + \frac{4}{3}x + 4}
\]

The equation of the circle with endpoints (-2, 1) and (6, -3) as its diameter can be found by determining the midpoint for the center and calculating the radius.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Find the Midpoint (Center):**
   The midpoint M between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is given by:
   \[
   M = \left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right)
   \]
   Plugging in the points (-2, 1) and (6, -3):
   \[
   M_x = \frac{-2 + 6}{2} = 2,\quad M_y = \frac{1 + (-3)}{2} = -1
   \]
   So, the center is at \( (2, -1) \).

2. **Calculate the Radius:**
   The radius is half the length of the diameter. First, find the distance between the two points using the distance formula:
   \[
   d = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
   \]
   Substituting the values:
   \[
   d = \sqrt{(6 - (-2))^2 + (-3 - 1)^2} = \sqrt{8^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{64 + 16} = \sqrt{80} = 4\sqrt{5}
   \]
   Therefore, the radius \( r = \frac{d}{2} = \frac{4\sqrt{5}}{2} = 2\sqrt{5} \).

3. **Formulate the Equation of the Circle:**
   The standard form of a circle's equation with center at (h, k) and radius r is:
   \[
   (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
   \]
   Substituting \( h = 2 \), \( k = -1 \), and \( r = 2\sqrt{5} \):
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y - (-1))^2 = (2\sqrt{5})^2 \\
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 4 \times 5 \\
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 20
   \]

**Final Answer:**

The equation of the circle is:
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 20
\]

The given equation \( x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 2y - 11 = 0 \) can be rewritten in the standard form of a circle by completing the square for both \( x \) and \( y \) terms.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Group like terms:**  
   \( (x^2 - 4x) + (y^2 + 2y) = 11 \)

2. **Complete the square for \( x \):**  
   Take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \( -4/2 = -2 \). Square it: \( (-2)^2 = 4 \). Add and subtract this inside the equation.

3. **Complete the square for \( y \):**  
   Take half of the coefficient of \( y \), which is \( 2/2 = 1 \). Square it: \( (1)^2 = 1 \). Add and subtract this as well.

4. **Rewrite with completed squares:**  
   \( (x^2 - 4x + 4) - 4 + (y^2 + 2y + 1) - 1 = 11 \)

5. **Simplify the perfect squares and combine constants:**  
   \( (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 - 5 = 11 \)

6. **Move constant to the right side:**  
   \( (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16 \)

**Final Answer:**
The equation in standard form is \( (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16 \).

   The standard form of a circle's equation is:
   \[
   (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
   \]
   where \((h, k)\) is the center and \(r\) is the radius.

   Given the center of the circle is at (3, 4), so:
   \[
   h = 3,\quad k = 4
   \]

3. **Determine the Radius:**
   Since the circle is tangent to the x-axis, the radius \(r\) is equal to the y-coordinate of the center because the distance from the center to the x-axis (y=0) must be equal to the radius.
   \[
   r = 4
   \]

   \[

   \]

To find the equation of a circle with center \((-2, 5)\) that passes through the point \((1, 9)\), we can use the standard form of the circle's equation:

\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]

Where:
- \((h, k)\) is the center of the circle.
- \(r\) is the radius.

**Step 1: Plug in the Center Coordinates**

The center of the circle is given as \((-2, 5)\). Substituting these values into the equation:

\[
(x - (-2))^2 + (y - 5)^2 = r^2
\]
\[
(x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = r^2
\]

**Step 2: Find the Radius Using the Given Point**

The circle passes through the point \((1, 9)\). To find the radius, we substitute \(x = 1\) and \(y = 9\) into the equation:

\[
(1 + 2)^2 + (9 - 5)^2 = r^2
\]
\[
3^2 + 4^2 = r^2
\]
\[
9 + 16 = r^2
\]
\[
25 = r^2
\]

So, \(r = \sqrt{25} = 5\).

**Step 3: Write the Complete Equation of the Circle**

Now that we have the radius squared (\(r^2 = 25\)), substitute it back into the equation:

\[
(x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 25
\]

This is the equation of the circle in standard form.

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{(x+2)^{2}+(y-5)^{2}=25}
\]

I'll start by recalling the general form of a circle's equation:
\[
x^2 + y^2 + Dx + Ey + F = 0
\]
where \(D\), \(E\), and \(F\) are constants that determine the circle's properties.

Next, I'll substitute each of the given points into this equation to create a system of equations.

For point (1, 1):
\[
1^2 + 1^2 + D(1) + E(1) + F = 0 \implies 1 + 1 + D + E + F = 0 \implies D + E + F = -2
\]

For point (2, -3):
\[
2^2 + (-3)^2 + D(2) + E(-3) + F = 0 \implies 4 + 9 + 2D - 3E + F = 0 \implies 2D - 3E + F = -13
\]

For point (5, -1):
\[
5^2 + (-1)^2 + D(5) + E(-1) + F = 0 \implies 25 + 1 + 5D - E + F = 0 \implies 5D - E + F = -26
\]

Now, I have a system of three equations:
\[
\begin{cases}
D + E + F = -2 \\
2D - 3E + F = -13 \\
5D - E + F = -26
\end{cases}
\]

I'll solve this system step by step.

Subtract the first equation from the second:
\[
(2D - 3E + F) - (D + E + F) = -13 - (-2) \\
D - 4E = -11 \quad \text{(Equation A)}
\]

Subtract the first equation from the third:
\[
(5D - E + F) - (D + E + F) = -26 - (-2) \\
4D - 2E = -24 \quad \text{(Equation B)}
\]

Now, I'll simplify Equation B by dividing both sides by 2:
\[
2D - E = -12 \quad \text{(Equation C)}
\]

From Equation A:
\[
D = -11 + 4E
\]

Substitute \( D \) from Equation A into Equation C:
\[
2(-11 + 4E) - E = -12 \\
-22 + 8E - E = -12 \\
7E = 10 \\
E = \frac{10}{7}
\]

Now, substitute \( E = \frac{10}{7} \) back into Equation A to find \( D \):
\[
D = -11 + 4 \times \frac{10}{7} = -11 + \frac{40}{7} = -\frac{37}{7}
\]

Finally, substitute \( D \) and \( E \) into the first equation to find \( F \):
\[
-\frac{37}{7} + \frac{10}{7} + F = -2 \\
-\frac{27}{7} + F = -2 \\
F = -2 + \frac{27}{7} = \frac{-14 + 27}{7} = \frac{13}{7}
\]

Now that I have \( D = -\frac{37}{7} \), \( E = \frac{10}{7} \), and \( F = \frac{13}{7} \), I can write the equation of the circle:
\[
x^2 + y^2 - \frac{37}{7}x + \frac{10}{7}y + \frac{13}{7} = 0
\]

To convert this into the standard form, I'll complete the squares for \( x \) and \( y \):

For \( x \):
\[
x^2 - \frac{37}{7}x = \left(x - \frac{37}{14}\right)^2 - \left(\frac{37}{14}\right)^2
\]

For \( y \):
\[
y^2 + \frac{10}{7}y = \left(y + \frac{5}{7}\right)^2 - \left(\frac{5}{7}\right)^2
\]

Substituting these back into the equation and simplifying, I'll find the center at \( \left( \frac{37}{14}, -\frac{5}{7} \right) \) and the radius as required.
</think>

To determine the equation of a circle passing through the points (1, 1), (2, -3), and (5, -1), follow these steps:

### Step 1: General Equation of a Circle
The general form of a circle's equation is:
\[
x^2 + y^2 + Dx + Ey + F = 0
\]
where \( D \), \( E \), and \( F \) are constants.

### Step 2: Substitute the Points into the Equation
Substitute each point into the general form to create a system of equations.

1. For (1, 1):
   \[
   1^2 + 1^2 + D(1) + E(1) + F = 0 \\
   2 + D + E + F = 0 \\
   D + E + F = -2
   \]

2. For (2, -3):
   \[
   4 + 9 + 2D - 3E + F = 0 \\
   13 + 2D - 3E + F = 0 \\
   2D - 3E + F = -13
   \]

3. For (5, -1):
   \[
   25 + 1 + 5D - E + F = 0 \\
   26 + 5D - E + F = 0 \\
   5D - E + F = -26
   \]

### Step 3: Solve the System of Equations
We now have:
\[
\begin{cases}
D + E + F = -2 \\
2D - 3E + F = -13 \\
5D - E + F = -26
\end{cases}
\]

Subtracting the first equation from the second and third equations to eliminate \( F \):

1. From the second equation:
   \[
   (2D - 3E + F) - (D + E + F) = -13 - (-2) \\
   D - 4E = -11 \quad \text{(Equation A)}
   \]

2. From the third equation:
   \[
   (5D - E + F) - (D + E + F) = -26 - (-2) \\
   4D - 2E = -24 \quad \text{(Equation B)}
   \]
   
Simplify Equation B by dividing by 2:
\[
2D - E = -12 \quad \text{(Equation C)}
\]

### Step 4: Solve for \( D \) and \( E \)
From Equation A:
\[
D = -11 + 4E
\]
Substitute \( D \) into Equation C:
\[
2(-11 + 4E) - E = -12 \\
-22 + 8E - E = -12 \\
7E = 10 \\
E = \frac{10}{7}
\]

Now, find \( D \):
\[
D = -11 + 4 \times \frac{10}{7} = -11 + \frac{40}{7} = -\frac{37}{7}
\]

Finally, substitute \( D \) and \( E \) into the first equation to find \( F \):
\[
-\frac{37}{7} + \frac{10}{7} + F = -2 \\
-\frac{27}{7} + F = -2 \\
F = -2 + \frac{27}{7} = \frac{-14 + 27}{7} = \frac{13}{7}
\]

### Step 5: Write the Equation of the Circle
Substituting \( D \), \( E \), and \( F \) into the general equation:
\[
x^2 + y^2 - \frac{37}{7}x + \frac{10}{7}y + \frac{13}{7} = 0
\]

To present it in a more standard form, multiply all terms by 7 to eliminate the denominators:
\[
7x^2 + 7y^2 - 37x + 10y + 13 = 0
\]

### Final Answer

The equation of the circle is:
\[
\boxed{7x^2 + 7y^2 - 37x + 10y + 13 = 0}
\]

Starting with the equation:
\(9x^2 + 4y^2 = 36\)

I want to simplify this by dividing each term by 36, which will transform the right-hand side into 1. This step helps in isolating \(x^2\) and \(y^2\) terms.

After division, the equation becomes:
\(\frac{9x^2}{36} + \frac{4y^2}{36} = 1\)

Simplifying each fraction further by dividing numerator and denominator by their greatest common divisors (GCDs):
For \(x^2\) term: GCD of 9 and 36 is 9, so it becomes \(\frac{x^2}{4}\).
For \(y^2\) term: GCD of 4 and 36 is 4, so it becomes \(\frac{y^2}{9}\).

Thus, the equation in standard form is:
\(\frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1\)

This means that \(a^2 = 4\) and \(b^2 = 9\), so \(a = 2\) and \(b = 3\). Here, since \(b > a\), the major axis is along the y-axis with length \(2b = 6\) units.

\text{Minor Axis} = 2a = 4
\]

**Final Answer:**
The standard form of the given equation is:
\[
\boxed{\frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1}
\]
with a major axis length of \(6\) and a minor axis length of \(4\).

The given problem involves an ellipse with foci at (40, 0) and (-40, 0), indicating a major axis length of 100 along the x-axis. 

To find the equation:
- The semi-major axis \(a\) is half the major axis: \(a = \frac{100}{2} = 50\).
- The distance from the center to each focus (\(c\)) is 40.
- Using the relationship \(c^2 = a^2 - b^2\), we find:
  \[
  b^2 = a^2 - c^2 = 50^2 - 40^2 = 2500 - 1600 = 900

- Therefore, \(b = 30\).

\]

\[

\]

\[

\]

b^2 = 1600

Therefore, b = 40.

Now that I have a and b, I can plug them back into the standard equation.

\[
\frac{x^2}{2500} + \frac{y^2}{1600} = 1
\]

Wait, another thought: since it's a horizontal major axis, 'a' is larger than 'b', which makes sense here because 50 > 40. Also, the foci are indeed located at (±c, 0), where c = √(a^2 - b^2) = √(2500 - 1600) = √900 = 30, which matches the given information.

To convert the given equation \(4x^2 - 9y^2 = 36\) into the standard form of a hyperbola, follow these steps:

**Step 1: Divide both sides by 36**
\[
\frac{4x^2}{36} - \frac{9y^2}{36} = \frac{36}{36}
\]

**Step 2: Simplify the fractions**
\[
\frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{4} = 1
